---
title: "Use cases"
description: "Company monitoring, industry intelligence, open-ended topic monitoring, and entity tracking."
---

### Company news : positive funding and deal activity

Fetch all positive news about a company over a date range, filtered to funding and M&A event types.

Use the following filters:

- sentiment\_list filter to fetch only positive news.
- type\_list filter - CM01, CM03 to filter only the news related to funding and M&A activity. Refer to the complete list of news type filters [here](/news-types).

<CodeGroup>

```bash cURL
curl --location 'https://api.akta.pro/api/v1/news?company=https%3A%2F%2Fcanva.com&sentiment_list=Positive&type_list=CM01%2C%20CM03' \
--header 'x-api-key: YOUR API KEY'
```

```python Python
import requests

url = "https://api.akta.pro/api/v1/news?company=https://canva.com&sentiment_list=Positive&type_list=CM01, CM03"

payload = {}
headers = {
  'x-api-key': 'Your API Key'
}

response = requests.request("GET", url, headers=headers, data=payload)

print(response.text)
```

</CodeGroup>

      "full_text": "Future of Work 10 digital products you can create once and sell forever These digital products to sell online let creators build once and collect income for years, from templates and courses to presets and stock media\n\n1 / 12 List slides 10 digital products you can create once and sell forever List slides Previous Start over By · Updated August 14, 2026 Add QZ to Google Credit: https://kaboompics.com/ / Pexels A digital product only has to be built once. It can then be sold to the 10th customer as easily as the first, which is what separates it from almost every other way of making money online. Digital products to sell online — files, templates, courses and other work that lives entirely on a screen — do not require inventory, shipping or a factory floor. Once a file is finished and uploaded to a marketplace or a personal store, it can be downloaded again and again. That can happen a hundred times or a hundred thousand times without the creator repeating the original work. That does not mean these products build themselves, or that they never need attention after launch. A course still needs updated slides when the software it teaches changes. A font still needs new weight variations if a foundry wants it to compete with established libraries. What changes is the relationship between effort and income. The bulk of the work happens up front. Every sale after the first one is close to pure profit, since the marginal cost of another download is close to zero. This list focuses on 10 categories that people are actually building and selling right now, not abstract ideas. Some, like e-books and online courses, require real subject-matter expertise and a fair amount of time to produce well. Others, like Lightroom presets or Canva templates, can be built from skills many people already have, using software many already own. What they share is a business model: create the file once, then distribute it indefinitely. A marketplace, a personal website or a simple checkout page can handle the transaction from there. None of these require a large existing audience to start. A handful of Etsy sales, a small course cohort or a modest download count on a stock media platform can validate an idea early. That validation can happen before a creator invests significant additional time. The rest of this guide walks through 10 specific product types and the platforms where each one is typically sold. It also covers what makes each one realistic to build without any prior experience running a business. Notion templates for productivity and small business systems Credit: appshunter.io / Unsplash Notion templates are pre-built pages inside the workspace app Notion, sold as a shareable link that a buyer duplicates into their own account. They range from simple habit trackers to full small-business operating systems. The appeal for a creator is structural. Notion lets anyone build a page using its own free interface. That page can then be published as a template through Notion's own template gallery, a personal storefront, or third-party marketplaces such as Gumroad. There is no software to code and no file format to manage beyond a link. A buyer clicks it, hits duplicate, and the entire structure — databases, linked pages, formulas and formatting — copies into their own account in seconds. Demand tends to cluster around a few categories. Freelancers and small agencies buy client management systems that track projects, invoices and deadlines in one place. Students and job seekers buy study planners and application trackers. Content creators buy editorial calendars that link scripts, deadlines and publishing status together. Small business owners buy inventory and order-tracking systems that replace a messy spreadsheet. The common thread is that Notion already has the audience, and a template saves a buyer the hours it would take to design that structure from scratch. Building one requires no special software beyond a free Notion account and a clear sense of a real workflow problem. The more valuable templates tend to solve a specific, named problem — a wedding planning system, a freelance tax tracker, a semester course planner. A generic \"productivity system\" with no particular user in mind tends to sell less well. Pricing on most platforms sits well under $50, since the product is a single license to duplicate a structure rather than ongoing software access. The main maintenance a template needs is occasional updates when Notion changes its own features. A template built on an older database format can look dated or even break in minor ways. Beyond that, a finished template can be sold indefinitely without the creator rebuilding it for each buyer, which is the core appeal of the format. Self-paced online courses sold through Teachable or Thinkific Credit: Kelly Huang / Unsplash A self-paced online course is a set of pre-recorded video lessons sold through a hosting platform such as Teachable, Thinkific or Kajabi. Students learn on their own schedule without live instruction from the creator. The format works because the platform, not the creator, handles the repetitive parts of running a school. Video hosting, student logins, payment processing and progress tracking are built into the software, so a creator's job is limited to recording the lessons once and organizing them into modules. After that, enrollment can happen at any hour without the creator present. That is different from a live cohort course or a coaching call, where the creator's time is tied to every student. The subjects that tend to sell well share a pattern: a skill with a clear before-and-after, taught by someone who can demonstrate it rather than only describe it. Software tutorials, creative skills like photography or illustration, language learning, and professional certifications for fields such as project management all fit this pattern. A course on a broad topic like \"productivity\" tends to compete with too much free content already on YouTube. A narrow course such as \"batch editing wedding photos in Lightroom\" competes with almost nothing. Production quality matters less than structure. Screen recordings made with free software such as OBS Studio can work well, paired with a clear outline of what each module teaches. A short assignment or checklist at the end of each section tends to perform better than expensively produced video built around a vague curriculum. Students buying a self-paced course are paying for a result and a clear path to it, not cinematography. The ongoing work after launch is real but limited. A creator might answer questions in a course community, update a lesson when the underlying software changes, or refresh an outdated screenshot. None of that requires the course to be rebuilt from scratch. Platforms typically take a percentage of each sale or charge a flat monthly hosting fee, which is the tradeoff for not building payment and hosting infrastructure independently. E-books on a narrow, specific topic Credit: @felirbe / Unsplash An e-book sold on a narrow, specific topic tends to outperform a broad one. A reader searching for an exact answer will pay for a short, focused text faster than a long general one. Self-published e-books can be distributed through Amazon $AMZN Kindle Direct Publishing, Gumroad, or a creator's own website, with formats ranging from a simple PDF to a formatted e-reader file. Unlike a course, an e-book requires no video equipment or recording software, only writing and, ideally, a round of editing. That lower production barrier is also why the category is crowded, which is why specificity matters more here than in almost any other digital product on this list. The topics that succeed commercially tend to solve a defined problem for a defined reader. Examples include a meal-prep guide for a specific diet, a grammar guide for a specific language pair, or a legal and tax explainer for a specific profession. Another common example is a guide focused on one specific software feature. A 15,000-word book that answers one question thoroughly generally sells better than a 60,000-word book covering a subject broadly. The buyer already knows what they want before they click purchase. Formatting affects perceived value more than length does. A plain document with inconsistent fonts and no table of contents reads as unfinished, even when the writing itself is strong. A clean layout with a table of contents, consistent headers and a simple cover signals that the content was made with care. Tools such as Canva, Vellum or even a well-formatted Google $GOOGL Doc exported to PDF can produce a professional result without any design software experience. Pricing for a niche e-book commonly falls between $5 and $25, depending on how specific and how immediately useful the content is to the buyer. Once written, edited and formatted, the file requires no further work to sell again. The main exception is occasional updates, needed if the underlying facts, laws or software the book describes change over time. Printable planners and worksheets sold on Etsy Credit: Kendra Wesley / Unsplash Printable planners and worksheets are digital files, usually PDFs, that a buyer downloads and prints at home. They remain one of the best-selling digital product categories on Etsy. The format sits between a physical product and a pure digital one. The buyer receives a file rather than a shipped item, so the seller avoids packaging and postage. The finished product still gets used on paper, though, which appeals to a specific kind of shopper who wants a printed planner without the cost of a commercially bound one. Categories that consistently find buyers include budget trackers, meal planners, and wedding planning packets. Chore charts for children and habit trackers built around a specific goal, such as training for a first five-kilometer race, also sell steadily. Design tools have lowered the barrier to entry sharply. Canva's grid and template system lets a creator lay out a multi-page planner without any graphic design training. Etsy's own seller tools handle the checkout and automatic file delivery once a design is uploaded. This means a single creator can list dozens of printable designs without needing separate software for each one, since the design and sales infrastructure are already built by the platforms. Differentiation in a crowded printable market usually comes from specificity and cohesive design, not novelty of concept. A generic weekly planner competes against thousands of similar listings. A planner built for a specific audience, such as freelance photographers tracking client shoots, or families managing a child's individualized education plan, faces far less direct competition. Bundling several related printables into one listing, such as a full set of budgeting worksheets sold together, also tends to raise the average order value. That bundling requires little additional design work beyond what a single sheet would take. Because the file is delivered automatically after purchase, a printable requires essentially no ongoing labor once it is designed. The main exception is occasional updates to keep the layout current with seasonal trends, or to add sizing for buyers who prefer to fill planners out digitally on a tablet. Stock photos and video clips licensed for commercial use Credit: Alexander Wang / Unsplash Stock photos and video clips are licensed images and footage sold through platforms such as Adobe $ADBE Stock, Shutterstock and Pond5. A single file can be licensed to hundreds of different buyers over time. Unlike most items on this list, stock media does not require teaching a skill or solving a workflow problem. It requires producing footage or images that businesses, marketers and publishers need regularly and would rather license than shoot themselves. Common subjects that stay in steady demand include office and workplace scenes, food close-ups, nature and landscape footage, and diverse groups of people in everyday settings. These cover the widest range of commercial uses in advertising and editorial content. Technical quality matters more here than in almost any other category on this list, since stock platforms reject submissions that fail basic standards for sharpness, lighting and file resolution. A modern smartphone camera can meet these standards for many subjects. Platforms increasingly favor 4K video and high-resolution raw photo files, though, for maximum licensing flexibility across print, web and broadcast use. Income in stock media is built on volume rather than a small number of high-value sales. A single photo might earn a modest royalty each time it is licensed. Successful contributors typically upload libraries in the hundreds or thousands of files across a range of subjects, rather than relying on a handful of images to generate meaningful income. Metadata — the keywords and descriptions attached to each file — affects how often a file appears in a buyer's search. That makes accurate, thorough tagging as important as the image itself. Model releases and property releases are required whenever a recognizable person or private property appears in a submission. Platforms will reject or later remove files that lack proper legal clearance. Once a file is approved and live on a platform, it continues generating royalties from new licenses without any further action from the creator. The main ongoing task is periodically refreshing an aging library with newer footage that reflects current fashion, technology and design trends. WordPress and Shopify themes built for a specific niche Credit: Balázs Kétyi / Unsplash WordPress and Shopify $SHOP themes are pre-built website designs that a buyer installs on their own site, giving a business a professional layout without hiring a web designer from scratch. Marketplaces such as ThemeForest, along with the official WordPress and Shopify theme stores, handle distribution, licensing and often customer support tiers. That infrastructure lets a single designer sell the same theme to thousands of different website owners. A theme built for WordPress typically needs to work with the block editor and common plugins for forms, e-commerce and search engine optimization. A Shopify theme instead needs to integrate with that platform's own checkout, inventory and app ecosystem. Niche-specific themes tend to outperform generic ones in a marketplace as saturated as ThemeForest. A general \"business theme\" competes against thousands of similar listings. A theme built specifically for restaurants, wedding photographers, dental practices or online course creators faces a smaller, more targeted pool of competitors instead. It can also be marketed directly to that audience through niche-specific communities and search terms. Building a sellable theme requires real front-end development skill, typically HTML, CSS, and either PHP for WordPress or Shopify 's Liquid templating language. It also requires an understanding of responsive design across phone, tablet and desktop screens. This is a higher technical bar than most items on this list. That is part of why individual themes often sell for $30 to $80, rather than the $5-to-$25 range typical of a printable or a template. The ongoing obligation with themes is heavier than with a static file. Both WordPress and Shopify update their core software regularly, and a theme that is not maintained can eventually stop working correctly or create security vulnerabilities for buyers. Many theme marketplaces require sellers to provide updates for a set period after purchase. Reputable sellers continue supporting a popular theme well beyond that window, to protect their reviews and repeat sales. Even with that maintenance cost, a single well-designed theme can generate sales for years without being rebuilt from scratch. Custom fonts sold through type foundries and marketplaces Credit: Elena Mozhvilo / Unsplash Custom fonts are typefaces designed and licensed for use in branding, print and digital design, sold through marketplaces such as Creative Market and MyFonts or through independent type foundries. A single font file can be licensed repeatedly to individual designers, small businesses, and in some cases large companies paying for extended commercial use. The type designer does not need to produce any new work after the initial design is finished. This licensing structure is what makes fonts one of the more durable digital products on this list. A well-designed typeface can remain in active commercial use for decades after its release. Designing a font that sells requires real typographic skill. That includes consistent letterforms across an entire character set, proper spacing between letter pairs known as kerning, and support for the accented characters needed for languages beyond English. Software such as Glyphs or FontLab is standard for professional type design, though free tools exist for designers building their first typeface at a smaller scale. This is a more specialized skill than most items on this list, closer to graphic design or lettering than to general content creation. Font styles that tend to sell consistently include handwritten and script fonts for wedding and greeting card design. Clean sans-serif fonts for branding and logo work also sell well, as do display fonts with strong personality for social media graphics and packaging design. Font families that include multiple weights, from light through bold, tend to command higher prices than a single-weight font, since they give buyers more flexibility across a single design project. Licensing terms matter as much as the design itself. Foundries typically sell separate licenses for personal use, commercial use, and app or software embedding. Pricing scales based on how the font will be used and how large the buyer's business is. Once those licensing tiers and the font files themselves are finished, a foundry can continue selling the same typeface indefinitely. The main additional work is periodic expansion into new language character sets or font weights, to keep a popular typeface competitive. Lightroom presets and video color grading LUTs Credit: Helena Jankovičová Kováčová / Pexels Lightroom presets and video LUTs are saved color-editing settings that a buyer applies to their own photos or footage with one click. They recreate a specific visual style without manual color correction. A preset for Adobe $ADBE Lightroom stores adjustments to exposure, contrast, color temperature and other settings as a small file that photographers install into their own editing software. A LUT, short for look-up table, does the same job for video footage and works across editing programs such as Adobe Premiere Pro, DaVinci Resolve and Final Cut Pro. Both formats let a photographer or video editor achieve a consistent, polished look in seconds, rather than adjusting each setting manually on every photo or clip. Wedding, travel and portrait photographers make up a large share of the buyer base. These photographers often shoot large volumes of images that need a consistent, recognizable editing style applied across an entire client gallery. Content creators making short-form video for platforms such as Instagram and TikTok drive much of the demand for video LUTs. A distinct visual style helps a creator's content stand out and stay consistent from post to post. Creating a sellable preset pack starts with developing an editing style that looks distinctive across a range of different lighting conditions and photo subjects. A preset that only looks good on one type of image has limited resale value. Sellers typically demonstrate this by showing before-and-after examples across several different photos, proving the preset performs consistently rather than relying on a single flattering example. Distribution happens through marketplaces such as Creative Market and Etsy, or through a creator's own website paired with a simple digital delivery service. Because the file itself is tiny, often just a few kilobytes of adjustment data, hosting and delivery costs are negligible. Once a preset pack is finished and tested across a range of images, it can be sold indefinitely without any further editing work from the creator. The main exception is occasional updates when Adobe or another company changes its editing software's underlying tools. Royalty-free music loops and sound effect packs Credit: Cristian Benavides / Pexels Royalty-free music and sound effect packs are audio files licensed for use in videos, podcasts and games. They are sold through platforms such as AudioJungle, Epidemic Sound and Pond5 without ongoing royalty payments to the creator each time a track is used. The licensing model is what separates this category from traditional music sales. A buyer pays once, or subscribes to a platform, for the right to use a track in their own project without needing to negotiate rights or pay performance royalties afterward. That is why the format appeals heavily to podcasters, YouTube creators and independent video producers working with limited budgets. A single well-made track or sound effect pack can be licensed to thousands of different creators over its lifetime. Instrumental tracks tend to perform better commercially than vocal tracks. Background music with lyrics can compete with a video's own dialogue or narration, which limits how a buyer can use the track. Genres in steady demand include upbeat corporate background music for advertising and presentations, and ambient or cinematic tracks for documentary-style content. Short musical stings used for transitions and intros across podcasts and YouTube videos also sell steadily. Sound effect packs occupy a related but distinct niche. Categories include user-interface clicks and notifications for app design, along with foley sounds like footsteps and door creaks for film and game production. Ambient background noise, such as city traffic or rainfall, rounds out the category for podcasts and video backgrounds. These packs often sell in bundles of dozens or hundreds of related sounds, since a single sound effect rarely justifies its own purchase. Producing commercially viable audio requires either music production training or genuine skill with production software such as Ableton Live, Logic Pro or FL Studio. It also requires understanding how tracks need to be structured for practical use, including versions with and without a strong ending so editors can loop or cut a track cleanly. Once mixed, mastered and uploaded to a licensing platform, a track continues generating income from new licenses without any further work from its creator. Canva templates for social media and marketing Credit: Jonathan Borba / Pexels Canva templates are pre-designed layouts built inside Canva's design platform, letting a buyer swap in their own text, photos and colors without starting a design from a blank page. Canva already has hundreds of millions of active users, so template sellers benefit from an existing audience that already knows the software. That is unlike a product that requires a buyer to learn a new tool before they can use it. Templates are typically sold through Canva's own Creator marketplace, through Etsy, or through a creator's personal website. They are delivered as a link that opens directly inside a buyer's own Canva account for editing. Instagram carousel posts, Pinterest $PINS pins, resume and cover-letter layouts, and small-business marketing materials such as flyers, menus and price lists are among the most consistent sellers. These are documents that a wide range of buyers need repeatedly but rarely have the design skill or time to build well from scratch. A small business owner without a design background, for instance, can buy a single branded template set and reuse the same layout structure for months of social media posts. Building a template that sells requires an eye for layout, color and typography more than technical software skill, since Canva's own interface handles most of the execution. Sellers typically build several variations within a single cohesive style, such as 10 different Instagram post layouts that share the same fonts and color palette. Buyers value a full set they can rotate through more than a single standalone design. Canva regularly updates its own features, and design trends shift over time. Template sellers who keep a library current tend to earn more than those who list a set once and never revisit it. That said, the core economics remain the same as everything else on this list. Once a template set is designed and listed, it can be sold to any number of buyers without being rebuilt. The seller's ongoing work becomes optional polish, rather than a requirement for every transaction."

      "full_text": "Artificial intelligence startups founded by Turkish entrepreneurs are emerging as success stories, with companies such as fal.ai and Periodic Labs surpassing the $1 billion valuation threshold through recent funding rounds. According to the 2025 Turkish Artificial Intelligence Ecosystem and Global Impact Report, Turkish AI entrepreneurship has evolved into an international ecosystem. The report estimates that 1,188 active AI startups currently operate within Türkiye. Including companies founded overseas by Turkish entrepreneurs, the total exceeds 1,400 startups. The ecosystem remains relatively young, with most companies established after 2020. Between 2024 and 2025, around 180 AI startups secured early-stage funding. One of the report's central conclusions is that Türkiye's AI ecosystem can no longer be measured solely within its borders. Data from industry monitor startups.watch treats startups founded in Istanbul alongside companies established in global technology hubs such as San Francisco by Turkish founders, offering a more comprehensive picture of the country's entrepreneurial footprint. Diaspora startups reach unicorn status Some of the ecosystem's most prominent success stories are now emerging from Silicon Valley. Founded in San Francisco in 2021 by Turkish engineers Burkay Gür and Görkem Yurtseven, fal.ai provides developers with APIs for deploying AI models that generate images, video and audio. In December 2025, the company raised $140 million in a Series D funding round backed by investors including Sequoia Capital and Nvidia, lifting its valuation to $4.5 billion. Its customer base includes technology companies such as Perplexity, Canva and Quora. The founders have indicated that an eventual U.S. public listing is among the company's long-term objectives. Another standout example is Periodic Labs, founded by former Google DeepMind researcher Ekin Doğuş Çubuk together with former OpenAI vice president Liam Fedus. The company combines artificial intelligence with automated robotics laboratories to accelerate materials science research. Although founded only last year, Periodic Labs raised $300 million in seed funding at a $1.3 billion valuation in September 2025. By March this year, its valuation had reportedly increased more than fivefold to approximately $7 billion, bringing it closer to decacorn status. AI becoming essential Ipek Çeliksöz, founder of CatalystX Global and investor, says Türkiye has seen the biggest surges in its history in the gaming sector since 2020. \"I think the chances of a startup that doesn't use AI surviving within five years are very low. In the U.S., 86% of startup investments in the first half of 2026 went to AI startups,\" said Çeliksöz. She said the trend is accelerating in Türkiye as well, stressing that the number of AI startups founded annually has increased by 137% since 2021, and the number of active startups has reached 1,500. \"Any startup that is global from day one and solves the customer's real problem in the most agile way using its own models and agents will become the 'Peak' of AI in its category.\" Bigger funding rounds Data from startups.watch shows that although the number of AI investment deals worldwide declined during 2025, the total value of funding reached a record high. The trend suggests venture capital firms are becoming increasingly selective, concentrating larger investments in companies viewed as potential market leaders rather than spreading capital across numerous early-stage startups. As of November 2025, there were 326 AI unicorns globally, with the overwhelming majority based in North America. Europe and Asia, meanwhile, are emerging as rapidly growing but increasingly competitive secondary hubs. Türk Telekom takes startups to Silicon Valley One of Türkiye's leading telecoms and technology companies, Türk Telekom, is expanding its efforts to help Turkish startups that have developed expertise in next-generation technologies and possess global growth potential reach global markets. Through its venture capital arm TT Ventures, the company has taken artificial intelligence and cloud-focused ventures to Silicon Valley for a program featuring training in the arena where AI startups are growing the fastest. TT Ventures launched the two-week program focused on \"Scaling and Globalization\" in San Francisco in cooperation with Stanford University's School of Engineering Global and Online Education Center. The effort is part of Türk Telekom's vision of bolstering the national technology initiative. Nine startups from the TT Ventures portfolio participated in workshops, mentoring sessions and networking events led by Stanford academics while also taking part in meetings and events across Silicon Valley and presenting their businesses during a Demo Day. Thanks to TT Ventures' strong global connections, the startups also had the opportunity to connect with an international network of investors and leaders from the world's leading technology companies. Startups participating in the program included Refabric, Priam AI, Homster, Zuzzuu, Werover, Milvus and Macellan Super App, all of which develop AI-based solutions. Over decade of support The international program serves as an extension of TT Ventures Pilot, the accelerator that Türk Telekom has been running for over 10 years. Under the Pilot program, startups receive mentoring, cash and investment support, office and infrastructure support and access to a broad customer network. They are also provided with comprehensive support ranging from strategic management consulting to business development. TT Ventures General Manager Muhammed Özhan. (Courtesy of TT Ventures) TT Ventures General Manager Muhammed Özhan said they not only invest in startups but also take strategic steps to help them gain a foothold on the global stage. Özhan noted that the program they designed in collaboration with Stanford is of great importance for the growth of these startups and their ability to create value on a global scale. Portfolio value tops $600 million Türk Telekom's efforts in this area are yielding tangible results. The total portfolio value of startups that have graduated from the Pilot program has exceeded $600 million. It demonstrates that the program has made a significant contribution to the company’s vision of transforming Türkiye into a country that produces and exports high-tech products. Fake airline, travel sites fuel rise in phishing attacks Planning a trip these days means juggling ride-sharing apps, airlines, and online travel agencies. This usually takes place across numerous tabs and devices, all while rushing to make sure you don’t miss out on the best offers. It is precisely this sense of urgency, combined with trust in familiar brands and heavy cross-platform traffic, that creates the perfect opportunity for cybercriminals to mimic legitimate transportation and travel services and offer phishing pages, fake apps and scam-oriented deals. Researchers from cybersecurity company Kaspersky examined threats spread by exploiting the names of leading transportation brands. Between the second quarter of 2025 and the first three-month period of 2026, Kaspersky detected 262,663 cyber incidents associated with these brands. Attacks impersonating the Emirates brand ranked first, accounting for 61% of all detections in the transportation category, followed by Uber at 37%. Cybercriminals are mimicing legitimate transportation and travel services and offer phishing pages, fake apps and scam-oriented deals. (Courtesy of Kaspersky) The fact that the overwhelming majority of activity observed in this category was carried out through these two industry-leading brands indicates that attackers are focusing their efforts on well-known services with large user bases and high payment traffic. Trojan attacks on rise Among files and objects associated with transportation brands, the most commonly detected threat type was Trojans (30.5%), followed by Banking Trojans (22.5%). This malware is designed specifically to steal banking login credentials and payment data. In other words, cybercriminals impersonating transportation brands are not just after reservation details; in the vast majority of cases, they are directly targeting the user’s bank account. One of the fraud methods identified by Kaspersky researchers is a classic scam scenario in which the scammers impersonate Irish low-cost carrier Ryanair and tell victims they are \"entitled to compensation.\" Victims receive a message stating that they are eligible for a compensation payment due to their flight. They are then asked to enter their account information or pay a small \"processing fee\" to receive the payment. The scam creates a strong sense of urgency to pressure victims into taking action, giving the impression that the so-called offer will expire in just a few seconds. This kind of extreme time pressure is a major red flag. Legitimate compensation claims are not time-limited in a way that forces people to make decisions within seconds, and airlines do not charge fees in advance for reimbursement. Monitoring communications In addition to transportation, Kaspersky also examined threats that impersonate travel and accommodation platforms. In the aforementioned period, 5,414 attack attempts associated with these brands were detected. The most commonly distributed threats under the guise of these services were once again Trojan horses, accounting for 54.6%. Travel platform accounts have become attractive targets because they often contain payment details, personal information, booking histories and conversations with hotels or service providers. Attackers use counterfeit booking sites, malicious attachments and fake applications to steal login credentials, compromise payment information, install additional malware or gain remote access to victims' devices.\n\nshortlink copied Last Update: Aug 07, 2026 6:36 pm"
    },

      "full_text": "Wordsmith AI has raised a $14 million extension to its Series B financing to expand its enterprise legal AI platform for in-house legal teams. The extension was led by new investor Intact Private Capital, with participation from existing investors Highland Europe and Index Ventures. FT Ventures also joined Wordsmith as a shareholder.\n\nThe financing follows a period in which Wordsmith reported 14-fold year-over-year revenue growth and expanded its customer base to more than 500 companies. Customers include BT, the Financial Times, Sage, Starling, Canva and Safelite.\n\nWordsmith plans to use the additional capital to deepen its presence in North America, where the company said demand from corporate legal departments is increasing.\n\nThe funding will also support further expansion across financial services and insurance, where legal teams require controlled and auditable systems for handling work in regulated environments.\n\nWordsmith is built specifically for in-house legal departments rather than law firms.\n\nThe company said corporate legal teams need technology that helps them resolve more work internally, reduce outside counsel spending and provide faster legal support across their organizations.\n\nWordsmith’s platform receives and routes legal requests, uses AI agents and approved playbooks to complete routine tasks, escalates matters requiring professional judgment and records each step of the process.\n\nThe company said enterprises with more than 10,000 employees are already using the platform.\n\nSome customers have reported seven-figure reductions in outside counsel spending within their first weeks of use.\n\nWordsmith also said legal teams that previously spent between 70% and 80% of their time on routine work have been able to redirect more of that time toward higher-value matters.\n\nRegulatory and multi-jurisdictional questions that may previously have been sent directly to outside counsel can now be assessed internally before external lawyers are engaged.\n\nThe Financial Times is both a customer and investor. Its in-house legal team selected Wordsmith as its enterprise legal AI platform, while FT Ventures participated in the financing.\n\nWordsmith positions its technology as a system of action for corporate legal departments, combining workflow automation, institutional knowledge and human review within one platform.\n\nKEY QUOTES:\n\n“In-house legal teams at financial services and insurance companies need legal infrastructure that can operate at enterprise scale while maintaining the control and auditability required in highly regulated environments.”\n\nJustin Smith-Lorenzetti, Managing Director at Intact Private Capital\n\n“FT’s own in-house legal team has chosen Wordsmith as its enterprise legal AI platform, providing the FT’s business faster access to legal support.”\n\nAlexandra Calinikos, Chief Investment Officer at FT Ventures\n\n“Requests come in, AI agents complete the routine work, lawyers approve what requires judgment, and every step is recorded.”\n\n“The result is a legal function that keeps more work in-house, spends less on external counsel and can finally show the business the impact it delivers.”\n\nRoss McNairn, CEO and Co-Founder of Wordsmith AI"
